科技股再度領跌，市場觀望 CPI 及台積電法說
2026 年 7 月 14 日
一、 7/13 科技股再度成為市場下跌核心，但資金並非全面撤出
1. 7 月 13 日美國半導體股再度成為市場主要賣壓來源 ， 跌幅明顯大於其他主要指數 。然
而，美國其他主要指數的跌幅相對較輕，顯示並不是所有產業同步崩跌，而是賣壓高
度集中於科技與半導體族群：
⚫ 道瓊工業指數下跌 138.37 點，跌幅 0.26%，收在 52,498.64 點；
⚫ 標普 500 指數下跌 60.05 點，跌幅 0.79%，收在 7,515.34 點；

⚫ Nasdaq 指數下跌 408.43 點，跌幅 1.55%，收在 25,873.18 點；
⚫ 費城半導體指數終場下跌約 4.78%，收在 12,347.78 點。
2. 以類股表現來看：資訊科技類股下跌 2.07%，為拖累大盤的主要來源；相較之下，受
惠於油價急升的能源類股上漲 3.16%，資金也轉向部分公用事業及金融股。
二、 下跌原因分析與市場看法：
1. 南韓記憶體類股表現疲弱，引發市場對 AI 疑慮：昨日半導體類股大跌，主要是記憶體
與 HBM 題材降溫引發市場重新評價。7 月 13 日南韓券商下修 SK Hynix 獲利展望
，並指出 HBM4 放量進度可能不如原先預期，使市場開始擔心記憶體漲價循環與
HBM 缺貨紅利是否已接近高峰，導致資金率先從前期漲幅最大的記憶體鏈出脫，SK
Hynix 重挫並拖累 Micron、SanDisk、Western Digital 等相關個股。這波賣壓也反
映投資人對 AI 資本支出能否持續擴張的疑慮：AI 需求雖強，但若未來算力供給快速

增加，GPU、HBM 與 AI 伺服器相關公司的稀缺性溢價可能被壓縮。再加上地緣政
治風險升溫推高油價與通膨預期，美債殖利率回升也使高 Beta 成長股承受估值壓力
，半導體在前期漲多、估值偏高的背景下自然成為獲利了結與風險調整的主要標的；
短線來看，SOX 尚未正式轉空，但已從強勢單邊上漲轉為高波動整理。
2. 資金尚未完全撤出科技類股：儘管科技類股為 7/13 下跌核心 ，輝達下跌 3.52%、AMD
下跌 4.21%、Marvell 下跌 7.75%；記憶體及儲存族群的壓力更為沉重，SanDisk 重
挫 12.63%，美光也下跌 4.32%。然而，Microsoft、Amazon 及 Apple 仍分別上漲
1.53%、0.80%及 0.63%。這三檔大型權值股逆勢走強，顯示即便先前漲多的硬體與
半導體業者股票遭逢賣壓，但市場資金也未全面撤離科技產業，而是由波動較大、估
值較高的半導體與 AI 硬體股，轉向現金流較穩定、業務較為分散的大型平台公司。

不另行通知。
3. 台積電法說會將成為全球科技類股風向球：台積電將於 7 月 16 日舉行第二季法說會
，在全球半導體股大幅震盪的情況下，預料投資人將等待公司公布第三季及全年指引
，以判斷後市科技類股發展。7 月 13 日台積電公布 6 月營收為新台幣 4,426.8 億元，
年增 67.9%；第二季營收約 1.27 兆元，年增約 36%，略高於市場預估的 1.264 兆元
，整體營收仍維持強勁，可以看出目前 AI 類股在基本面上仍然穩健，近期股價出現震
盪更多的是與估值壓縮以及市場情緒轉趨保守有關。
4. 美伊再度交火，能源價格反彈帶動升息預期攀升：美國於 7 月 13 日宣布恢復封鎖伊

朗港口，顯示美伊臨時停火已實質瓦解，雙方重新進入直接交火。此輪衝突主因伊朗
要求通行荷莫茲海峽的商船需取得其核准並使用伊朗指定航線，但部分船隻未依規定
通行而遭到攻擊，美國自 7 月 7 日起恢復空襲，伊朗隨即攻擊區內美軍設施，戰事於
7 月 11 日至 13 日進一步升級，帶動油價反彈至 80 美元左右水準，隨能源價格走升
，再度帶動市場升息預期上揚。高盛最新報告指出，預估美伊雙方最終最有可能的是
形成不穩定的妥協，由美國默許商船使用伊朗控制的航線並接受其通行協調，以換取
商業航運逐步恢復，在此情境下預期布蘭特原油價格將維持在每桶 75 至 85 美元；極
端情境下，若後續美伊戰火擴及區域能源設施，或荷莫茲海峽與曼德海峽同時中斷，
油價才可能再度突破 100 美元。
5. 投資人在 CPI 公布前保持觀望，高盛預估今年內利率不變：除了油價大漲與 AI 類股走
弱外，投資人也在美國 7 月 14 日公布 6 月消費者物價指數前保持觀望。高盛報告指
出，由於美國勞動市場仍維持穩健，通膨將是聯準會決定下一步政策的主要依據。高

盛預估 6 月核心 CPI 將月增 0.17%，整體 CPI 則因能源價格回落而將月減 0.11%；高
盛認為，整體 CPI 可能出現負值，不只是單月通膨下降，也代表先前由戰爭與油價上
漲帶來的通膨上行風險已明顯消退。依照高盛預估，6 月核心 CPI 年增率將為 2.76%
；若扣除高盛估算的關稅推升效果，核心 CPI 年增率則將接近 2%，此一增幅仍足以
使聯準會在 7 月維持利率不變。展望後續數月，高盛預估核心 CPI 月增率約為 0.20%
，主要反映住房通膨持續放緩，以及航空燃油與世界盃對旅遊服務價格的推升效果消
退，故聯準會在今年內按兵不動仍是高盛的基準情境，後續若伊朗衝突再度明顯升級
並推高油價，部分官員才可能因供給衝擊反覆出現而轉向支持升息。

三、 投資建議： 
1. 短線聚焦美股財報，科技股中期向上趨勢料不致有變：美股第二季財報季即將登場，
大型金融機構將率先揭露營運成果，此外，台積電法說(7/16)亦為科技股後續表現的
關鍵觀察重點，其對 AI 需求及資本支出的看法將影響市場對 AI 產業評價。區域市場
方面，建議持續關注與AI 供應鏈連動度較高的台灣及日本股市，其表現可望受惠於全
球 AI 資本支出循環延續。 
2. 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：在未來聯準會減少前瞻指引、利率路徑更依
賴數據環境下，市場對聯準會政策預期的調整頻率將提高，預期債券市場波動性亦將
隨之升溫。投資人對 Fed 升息疑慮或導致短天期債券殖利率出現較大波動；長天期債
券又有期限溢酬偏高、政府財政惡化及公債供給增加等風險。因此，建議債券布局可
聚焦 3 至 10 年期美國公債及投等債，不僅能提供相對具吸引力的息收，若未來通膨
預期放緩，在資本利得方面亦可望有所表現。

金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 7 月 27 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 川普關稅政策再掀波瀾：川普政府於 7/23 宣布替代版本,將於 7/24 零時生效,對美國主要
貿易夥伴課徵 10%–12.5% 稅率,涵蓋美國逾 99.4% 的進口品項;7/20 川普另依 1930 年
關稅法 Section 338 簽署三份公告,對加拿大酒類、曲棍球棍、水泥等特定商品加徵 50%
額外關稅,並同步啟動鋁冶煉廠投資獎勵計畫。
◼ ECB 利率按兵不動，委員關注中東能源二輪效應： ECB 於今年 6 月才因中東衝突與能源價

格壓力宣布升息一碼，7 月採「鷹派按兵不動」策略。理事會聲明強調當前能源價格「仍高
度波動、遠高於中東衝突前水位」 、且能源衝擊對通膨的完整二輪效應「尚未完全顯現」 。市
場當日對 9/10 ECB 再度升息機率定價約 70%。
◼ 胡塞攻擊推升油價，美伊暫停交火後市場降溫，但風險仍未根除：胡塞武裝 7/23 攻擊兩艘
沙國油輪，25 日再襲擊沙國紅海沿岸石油設施，推升能源運輸中斷疑慮。惟美伊 25 日至 26
日暫停相互攻擊，27 日亞洲盤油價回落逾 5%，美債殖利率亦降至 4.63%。但荷莫茲海峽與
紅海航運仍受阻，地緣政治溢價尚未完全消退。
市場展望與操作策略
◼ AI 支出與油價上揚引發疑慮，惟基本面料可支撐股市前景：上週 Alphabet 與 Tesla 財報引發
市場疑慮，中東局勢發展亦加重通膨與股市壓力。然而，台積電 Q2 營收達財測上緣，並將全
年美元營收成長展望上調至略高於 40%，強調 AI 需求依舊強勁；此外，Google Cloud 未交
付訂單增至 5,140 億美元，顯示 AI 前景仍具支撐。本波修正較偏向高估值與資金面的重新定

價，尚非 AI 基本面反轉，建議利用拉回分批布局具獲利潛力的科技股及台、日股市。
◼ 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：隨著 3～10 年期殖利率同步回升至具吸引力水準，
現階段可望兼具較高債息收益與適度存續期間效益 ； 債券天期過短面臨再投資風險 ， 超長天期
債又較易受財政赤字、公債供給及期限溢酬影響，建議持續以 3～10 年期美國公債搭配投資
級債，兼顧收益、防禦與未來利率回落時的資本利得機會。

### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 美國總統川普宣布同意與伊朗重啟談判 ， 但明確終止先前的停火協議 。 在卡達居
中斡旋下 ， 雙方正就霍爾木茲海峽航行權展開緊急協商 。 與此同時 ， 聯合國國際
海事組織 （IMO） 強烈譴責伊朗單方面設立機構管控海峽 ， 並呼籲各國拒絕承認
其主權宣稱 ， 然遭德黑蘭嚴詞拒絕 。 地緣政治緊張局勢推升本週國際油價上漲約
5%，市場擔憂能源成本攀升將加劇通膨壓力，中東戰火依舊反覆不定。
⚫ 美國聯準會上週向國會提交新任主席華許上任後的首份半年度貨幣政策報告。
報告指出，美國春季通膨進一步攀升，5 月 PCE 物價指數漲幅約為 2%目標的兩
倍，仍處高位。不過另一方面，勞動市場已趨於穩定，6 月失業率維持在 4.2%
的低位。強勁的勞動生產力帶動美國上半年 GDP 實現 2.1%的溫和擴張。
值得注意的是，報告將 AI 列為短期通膨推手，並自 2016 年來首度關注貨幣供
給量 （M2） ， 指出其增速已回落至2010 年代水準 ， 這可能成為抑制通膨的理由 。

針對利率路徑 ， 雖然多項政策規則暗示有升息必要 ， 但報告呼籲應謹慎解讀 。 華
許將於本週赴國會作證，市場將密切關注其對進一步貨幣政策的具體指引。
⚫ 據消息人士透露，受油價下跌影響，日本央行擬於 7 月底政策會議下調本財年
核心通膨預估（原估 2.8%） ，並因AI 強勁需求將經濟成長預測自 0.5%小幅上
修。儘管下調通膨預期，日銀對物價上行風險的警戒並未退燒。決策層評估，弱
勢日圓 、 薪資增長與前期進口成本的滯後效應 ， 將持續支撐通膨壓力 。 預期日銀
7 月將按兵不動，維持利率於 1%，並延續現有政策指引。為保留彈性，央行將
避免給出明確升息時間表，後續緊縮步伐將高度取決於夏季物價實際漲幅。

然而，出口商略顯惜售心態，在外資持續匯出之下，匯價進一步走低至 32.185；週
三，投信轉向匯入，外資匯出力道亦有所減緩，台幣由貶轉升，一度收復32.000 整
數大關 ， 最高觸及31.963； 週四外資已連續第六日賣超台股 ， 並因台積電發放股利 ，
外資加大匯出力道，台幣最低貶至 32.212，終場央行調節之下收在 32.178。

    預估本週台幣走勢偏弱。就國際市場而言，市場對聯準會升息的預期趨緩，惟
美伊衝突升溫 ， 避險需求支撐美元指數維持在101.000 上下震盪 ； 就台灣市場來看 ，
外資持續賣超台股，加以七月為股利發放旺季，股款及股利同步匯出之下，台幣承
受貶值壓力，惟匯價逢高有望吸引出口商拋匯，央行亦提供流動性，部分減緩台幣
貶值速度，綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢偏弱。

於各天期均線之上，且均線向上發散仍屬於多頭格局，然而匯價接近 163 整數關卡

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週歐元小幅走跌。週初，EUR/USD 大致在 1.1405 至 1.1445 間震盪；週間，
受到美國總統川普表示與伊朗臨時協議終止的言論及中東局勢升溫影響，帶動
EUR/USD 走跌最低來到 1.1390，後回升至 1.14 整數關卡之上波動；臨近週末，日
本政府表示將鼓勵養老基金投資國內資產的言論帶動日圓急升、美元指數走弱，
EUR/USD 最高來到 1.1460 後回落，終場收在 1.1413，週線下跌 22 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週公佈歐元區6 月份 PPI
月增 0.2%，年增 5.9%大致符合預期，而德國及法國 6 月份 CPI 分別為年增 2.4%及
2.0%均低於前值 ， 顯示通膨壓力有所放緩 ， 將減輕歐洲央行升息壓力 ， 不利EUR/USD
上行。二、上週多位歐洲央行官員發表談話，其中歐洲央行執委施納貝爾 (Isabel 
Schnabel)表示儘管油價回落當前物價仍未回到之前水平 ， 仍須關注通膨第二輪效應 ，
而歐洲央行管委史托納拉斯(Yannis Stournaras)表示，美伊爆發新一輪衝突使能源價
格再度上漲，將使歐洲央行對抗通膨的努力回到原點，顯示歐洲央行對於通膨仍保
持謹慎態度，若歐洲央行維持鷹派將為歐元帶來支撐。三、技術面而言，在日線圖
中 EUR/USD 匯價仍處於各天期均線下方 ， 雖一度突破月線但並未站穩 ， 顯示1.1450
上方賣壓沉重，而在 1.14 整數關卡之下則有買盤支撐，因此在有效突破前仍以區間
看待。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間震盪可能性較高。

### Page 1

韩国股市暴跌令约 70 万散⼾深陷杠杆 ETF ⾎亏，政府救市压⼒陡增。稳市基⾦、卖空禁
令、限制杠杆 ETF 、调整保证⾦要求、放宽回购规则 “ 五⼤救市牌 ” ⻬上桌，但如何稳市场⼜
避免道德⻛险，仍是最⼤考验。
摘要：
韩国股市 7 ⽉遭遇历史性暴跌，约 70 万散⼾投资者在杠杆 ETF 爆仓中损失惨重，政府正⾯临空前
政治压⼒，当局已着⼿评估多项市场⼲预⼯具。
Kospi 指数在本轮暴跌中接连跌破 50 ⽇、 100 ⽇和 200 ⽇均线，杠杆 ETF 资产规模从 6 ⽉峰值逾
500 亿美元骤降⾄约 160 亿美元，跌幅近 70% 。
据彭博等媒体周四报道，韩国市场当局正权衡更为激进的稳市⽅案，包括启⽤国家稳市基⾦、
卖空禁令、限制杠杆 ETF 。周三，韩国企划财政部已召开 " 紧急市场状况研判会议 " ，宣布维持最
⾼级别市场警戒，并启动跨部⻔ 24 ⼩时监测机制。
此次危机的政治敏感性尤为突出。
韩国政府此前曾主动⿎励散⼾参与三星电⼦和 SK 海⼒⼠主导的 AI 芯⽚股热潮，如今散⼾在 48 ⼩

时内遭受巨额亏损，令当局陷⼊两难。
Ethos Investment Management ⾸席投资官 James Fletcher 表⽰， " 当你引导家庭⼊市，⽽他们
随后在 48 ⼩时内蒙受如此规模的损失，采取⾏动的政治压⼒便会变得极为强烈。 "
杠杆 ETF 成重灾区，散⼾损失触⽬惊⼼
本轮暴跌的核⼼导⽕索，是 5 ⽉推出的挂钩三星电⼦和 SK 海⼒⼠的杠杆 ETF 产品。这类产品设
计⽤于放⼤标的股票的涨跌幅，在市场上⾏期吸引⼤批散⼾涌⼊，却在暴跌中将亏损成倍放
⼤。
据英国《⾦融时报》报道，韩国投资证券（ Korea Investment & Securities ）披露，其 88 万名持
有三星股票的客⼾中，近半数⽬前处于亏损状态； 40.8 万名持有 SK 海⼒⼠的投资者中，亏损⽐
例更⾼达近 70% 。
摩根⼤通数据显⽰，杠杆 ETF 资产规模从 2026 年初不⾜ 100 亿美元，急速膨胀⾄ 6 ⽉的逾 500 亿
美元，随后在 7 ⽉底骤降⾄约 160 亿美元，峰值⾄今跌幅接近 70% 。

⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

Pictet Asset Management 驻伦敦⾼级投资经理 Young Jae Lee 指出，散⼾⼤量买⼊杠杆 ETF 推
⾼波动率，同时迫使外资基⾦⼤举抛售， " 对散⼾⽽⾔，这是⼀场两败俱伤的游戏。 "
五⼤救市⼯具浮出⽔⾯
据彭博梳理，韩国当局⽬前正评估五项潜在⼲预⼿段。
国家稳市基⾦⽅⾯，政府已预留约 10 万亿韩元（约合 69 亿美元）⽤于市场动荡时期的股市稳
定，该⼯具上⼀次实际动⽤可追溯⾄ 2008 年⾦融危机。
不过， Indosuez Wealth Management 亚洲⾸席策略师 Francis Tan 警告，贸然动⽤存在道德⻛
险， " 虽然韩国政府可以通过股市稳定基⾦提供定向流动性、帮助恢复信⼼，但这有可能扭曲市
场信号。
" 国⺠年⾦公团（ NPS ）作为全球最⼤养⽼基⾦之⼀，通常与政府协同⾏动，但 Yuanta
Securities Korea 股票研究主管 Choi HyunJae 认为， NPS 主动介⼊的可能性不⼤，因为其国内股
票配置⽐例已超出战略⽬标上限。

卖空禁令⽅⾯，据韩联社 7 ⽉ 30 ⽇援引知情⼈⼠报道，韩国交易所（ KRX ）已在内部评估暂停卖
空的技术可⾏性及系统准备时间，同时检视将现⾏ 30% 涨跌停板幅度收窄的可操作性。
知情⼈⼠强调，上述⼯作属于针对极端⾏情的预防性评估，并不意味着相关措施即将落地。韩
国交易所随后公开否认，称未收到政府任何关于暂停卖空的要求，也未开展相关研究，并强调
此类政策并⾮交易所能够⾃⾏决定。
Fibonacci Asset Management ⾸席执⾏官 Jung In Yun 表⽰，卖空禁令应被视为 " 最后⼿段 " ，因
为 " 它可能在不解决根本问题的情况下损害外资信⼼ " 。
限制杠杆 ETF⽅⾯，部分投资者和议员呼吁将相关产品强制退市，甚⾄有投资者团体在国会⼤⻔
前摆放花圈以⽰抗议。
韩国顶级市场监管机构已对相关产品的推出表⽰ " 遗憾 " ，但强制退市⽬前看来可能性不⼤。当局
已采取临时措施，包括暂停新产品上市、限制投资者持仓上限并提⾼交易成本。
保证⾦要求⽅⾯，散⼾⼤量借贷买股、价格下跌后遭强制平仓，加剧了市场波动。

Tan 表⽰，政府可能考虑收紧保证⾦要求。 Choi 则建议，券商可适当放宽抵押品要求或给予短暂
宽限期，以缓解机械性抛售压⼒，但他同时指出，此举将使券商承担更⼤⻛险并压缩保证⾦相
关收⼊。
股票回购规则⽅⾯，政府还可能调整回购规定，⿎励已宣布回购计划且认为股价被低估的企业
加快购股节奏。现⾏规则对企业单次回购规模设有上限，且须按预先披露的时间表分散执⾏。
政治压⼒倒逼救市，道德⻛险争议犹存
分析⼈⼠普遍认为，鉴于政府此前主动⿎励散⼾参与 AI 和存储芯⽚热潮，当局最终出⼿⼲预的概
率正在上升。
James Fletcher 表⽰， " 对于⼀个积极⿎励散⼾⼊市的政府⽽⾔，这是⼀股强劲的逆⻛。 "

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

주: K-IFRS연결요약재무제표
[투자포인트]
■ 삼성전자의 2Q26 영업이익 전망치를 기존의 76.1조원에서 80.0조원으로 상향 조정한다. 또한 당사는 FY26
영업이익에 대한 전망치도 기존의 340조원에서 360조원으로 상향했다. 이는 2Q26과 3Q26의 DRAM, NAND
ASP 상승폭이 확대되기 때문이다. 당사의 채널 체크에 따르면 2Q26 DRAM Blended ASP는 40% 이상,
NAND ASP는 60%대 중반 상승한 것으로 보이며, 3Q26 DRAM, NAND ASP는 15~20% 가량 추가 상승할

가능성이 높은 것으로 판단된다.
■ 1Q26, 2Q26의 반도체 부문 직원들에 대한 상여금 지급용 충당금이 한꺼번에 반영될 2Q26의 동사 영업이익은
80.0조원으로 예상된다. 2Q26 메모리 반도체 부문에서는 79.2조원의 영업이익이 발생할 전망이며 충당금이
없었다면 94.9조원에 달했을 것으로 추정된다. 비메모리 부문에서도 Foundry 실적 개선에 따라 영업적자 폭이
소폭 감소했을 것으로 보인다. 반면 SDC, MX/NW 부문의 2Q26 영업이익은 메모리 가격 급등에 의한 부작용에
따라 0.2조원, 1.0조원으로 축소될 전망이다.
■ 메모리 반도체 업황은 적어도 CY27까지는 호황이 이어질 것으로 판단된다. CY27에는 DRAM 업계 생산
증가율이 올해의 25%에서 20% 미만으로 하락하고, 빅테크 업체들이 Cash 부족에도 불구하고 유상 증자, SPV
& JV 등의 방식으로 데이터센터 투자를 강하게 지속할 전망이기 때문이다.

■ 최근 급등에 따른 단기 주가 조정이 나타나고 있으나, 업황 및 실적 호조가 지속되고 글로벌 유동성 YoY
증감률의 상승이 2H26에 재개될 전망이므로 동사 주가의 중기 상승세는 유효할 것으로 판단된다.

### Page 2

2
– 직원들에 대한 신규 보상 체계를 감안한 삼성전자의 FY26 영업이익은 360조원으로 YoY 725% 증가할 전망이며 이에 기반한 FY26 예상 BPS는
10.3만원, ROE는 49%로 추정된다.
– 동사 FY26 예상 ROE는 지난 30년간 최고점이었던 FY00의 41%를 상회하므로, 동사 주가에 FY00 당시 동사 고점 P/B 배수 4.2배를 넘는 4.7 ~
5.0배의 적용이 가능하다.
– 보수적 관점에서 4.7배를 FY26 예상 BPS에 적용하면 48만원의 목표주가가 도출되며 CY27의 메모리 가격 상승 지속 여부에 따라 동사 주가가 이 이
상에서 추가 상승할 수 있을지가 결정될 것으로 판단된다.
– 단 이란 전쟁과 같은 지정학적 이슈가 재발하고 장기화될 경우, 스태그플레이션이 발생하고 고금리, 유동성 축소, 경기 악화에 따라 AI 투자 및
Memory 반도체 업황도 둔화될 것이므로 Val. 배수 및 목표주가의 하향 조정이 불가피할 수 있다.

당보고서는기관투자가및제3자에게E-mail등을통하여사전에배포된사실이없습니다.
회사는6개월간해당기업의유가증권발행과관련주관사로참여하지않았습니다.
당보고서에게재된내용들은본인의의견을정확하게반영하고있으며,외부의부당한압력이나간섭없이
작성되었음을확인합니다.
본분석자료는투자자의증권투자를돕기위한참고자료이며,따라서,본자료에의한투자자의투자결과에대해
어떠한목적의증빙자료로도사용될수없으며,어떠한경우에도작성자및당사의허가없이전재,복사또는
대여될수없습니다.무단전재등으로인한분쟁발생시법적책임이있음을주지하시기바랍니다.

Document 227: [373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다LG에너지솔루션(373220) 그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다LG에너지솔루션(373220) 그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-07-08
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

(373220) 
LG 에너지솔루션 
2026.07.08

에너지/인프라/배터리 황성현, Ph.D. 
6878 / tjdgus2009@eugenefn.com

그래도 꿈과 희망이 있다 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 470,000원(하향) 
 현재주가 332,000원(7/7)

 [투자의견] 당사는 LG에너지솔루션에 대해 목표주가를 470,000원으로 
15% 하향하며, 투자의견 BUY를 유지한다. 북미 EV 파우치 가동률 부진과 
ESS 신규 공장 초기 램프업 비용을 반영해 ‘26년 영업이익을 1조원에서 
6,300억원으로 하향 조정했기 때문이다. 그러나 유럽 전기차 판매량이 증
가세를 지속하고 있고 3Q26부터 GM 공장 가동 재개로 북미 EV 물량도 
점진적으로 회복될 전망이다. 동사는 Tesla와 43억달러 규모의 LFP 배터리 
공급계약을 확보했고, 한화큐셀과도 5GWh 규모의 ESS 배터리 공급계약을 
체결해 2024년 4.8GWh 계약에 이어 추가 물량을 확보했다. 이외에도 최
근 DTE에너지와 6GWh, 16억달러 규모의 ESS 공급계약을 체결하는 등 북
미 ESS 수주 모멘텀이 AI 데이터센터와 대형 전력망 프로젝트로 확대되고 
있다. 북미 ESS 생산능력도 ‘26년말 50GWh+로 확대될 예정이며, EV 수
요 부진으로 유휴화된 생산거점을 ESS로 전환해 신규 투자 부담을 낮추면
서 빠르게 캐파를 확보하고 있다. 단기 실적 부진은 지속되고 있으나 유럽 
EV 수요 회복, GM 공장 가동 재개, 46시리즈 출하 증가와 북미 ESS 가동 
본격화를 고려하면 실적과 주가의 바닥은 지나고 있다고 판단한다. 
 [2Q26 Review] 매출액 7.5조원(+15%qoq, +25%yoy), 영업이익 1,133
억원(흑전qoq, -77%yoy)으로 당사 추정을 하회하는 부진한 실적을 기록했
다. AMPC는 북미 ESS 생산량 증가로 2,410억원(+27%qoq)이 반영되었
으며, 이를 제외한 영업이익은 -1,277억원으로 3개 분기 연속 영업손실을 
기록했다. 1) 원통형 전지는 2170 및 46시리즈 물량이 동반 증가하며 가동
률이 70%를 상회해 매출액 2.2조원, 영업이익 1,673억원(영업이익률 
+7.5%)을 달성한 것으로 추정된다(KITA 원통형 마진 스프레드 36달러
/kWh로 전분기 대비 5달러/kWh 개선). 2) EV 파우치는 유럽향 물량 회복
에도 GM 공장 가동 중단 영향으로 매출액 3.4조원, 영업손실 797억원(영
업이익률 -2.4%)을 기록한 것으로 추정된다. 관세 환급 반영에도 마진 스프
레드는 20달러/kWh로 여전히 고정비가 커버되지 않는 상황이다. 3) ESS는 
매출액 2.0조원, 영업이익 260억원(영업이익률 +1.3%)으로 추정한다. 신
규 공장 가동과 북미 출하량 증가로 외형 성장을 지속했으며, 2Q AMPC 증
분의 대부분을 차지한 것으로 보인다. AMPC 포함 시 흑자를 기록했으나 초
기 램프업 비용과 팩 조립 병목 영향으로 이를 제외한 수익성은 부진했다. 
 [3Q26 Preview] 매출액 7.9조원(+4%qoq, +39%yoy), 영업이익 2,113
억원(+87%qoq, -65%yoy)로 전망한다. 3Q부터 GM 공장 가동이 재개되며 
1~2Q 부진했던 북미 EV 파우치 물량이 회복되고, 유럽향 물량도 점진적으
로 증가할 전망이다. ESS는 초기 램프업 비용과 팩 조립 병목이 완전히 해
소되지 않아 3Q에도 AMPC를 제외한 흑자 전환은 어려울 것으로 예상된
다. ESS의 본격적인 수익성 턴어라운드 시점은 4Q26으로 예상된다.

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에 동 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율 
종목추천 및 업종추천 투자기간: 12개월  (추천기준일 종가대비 추천종목의 예상 목표수익률을 의미함) 당사 투자의견 비율(%) 
ㆍSTRONG BUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +50%이상 3% 
ㆍBUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +15%이상 ~ +50%미만 94% 
ㆍHOLD(중립) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%이상 ∼ +15%미만 3% 
ㆍREDUCE(매도) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%미만 0% 
 (2026.06.30 기준)

과거 2년간 투자 의견 및 목표주가 변동내역 LG에너지솔루 션(373220.K S) 주가 및 목표주가 추이

Document 304: FX Daily_ Bond vigilantes may be the dollar’s best friends

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ Bond vigilantes may be the dollar’s best friends.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

Article | 30 July 2026 1
THINK economic and financial analysis
Article  |  30 July 2026   FX
FX Daily: Bond vigilantes may be the
dollar’s best friends
US real yields and the dollar dipped on yesterday's FOMC, where a
somewhat confusing press conference led investors to conclude that the
Fed was trying to avoid tightening. But the sell-off in the long end of the
Treasury market, pushing mortgage rates to new highs, will keep the Fed
on its guard. Look out for US PCE inflation, eurozone GDP and the BoE

today
A drop in real yields has weighed on the dollar
USD: Lots of focus on real yields
Last night's FOMC press conference was a little confusing. Looking at the market's reaction, the
conclusion was that the Fed was not going to be as tough on fighting inflation as initially
thought and might try to wriggle through this period of high inflation without hiking. Chair
Kevin Warsh's celebration of higher real yields and the more 'direct' message from the

markets was taken as a view that the Fed had outsourced monetary tightening to the
markets, reducing the need for hikes. To be honest, we are not sure if that is the correct read
or whether Warsh implicitly acknowledged that higher real rates embodied an expectation
that the Fed would hike in September.

### Page 2

THINK economic and financial analysis
Article | 30 July 2026 2
But the market reaction clearly reflected a Fed potentially going soft on inflation, with a huge
14bp steepening in the 2-30 year Treasury curve and US 30-year mortgage rates pushing
above 6.70%. Break-even inflation rates derived through the 5Y5Y inflation swap rose 6bp as
well.
Having risen 60bp since the June FOMC meeting, two US real yields fell 7bp yesterday and
undermined the dollar. Presumably, we will not receive much of a steer from the Fed before its

September meeting, and it will be the data which determines whether the Fed will hike. Bond
vigilantes could also have a say as well, if a sell-off in the long-end accelerates and the Fed is
forced to anchor the short-end with a hike.
For today, the focus will be on the first look at 2Q GDP data (expected at 2.0% QoQ annualised)
and the core PCE inflation data for June. The latter is expected to have slowed a little, with core
PCE at 0.2% month-on-month and the year-on-year rate dropping to 3.3% from 3.4%. Any

downside surprises here could hit the dollar given the emerging view that the Fed is trying to
avoid tightening.
DXY probably risks a correction back to the 100.50 area and the two sets of CPI prints and jobs
data before the 16 September FOMC meeting will determine whether DXY has topped for the
year.
Chris Turner
EUR: GDP and CPI data in focus
EUR/USD enjoyed a modest bounce after the Fed press conference, but probably got caught in
the crosscurrents. The drop in short-dated US real yields was a clear EUR/USD positive, but the

sell-off in long-dated US rates and the pressure it put on growth stocks in the S&P 500
probably capped the EUR/USD move. Ultimately, the ING house call is that the Fed does not
hike in September and EUR/USD ends the third quarter near 1.17. But high energy prices and a
strong US economy make that Fed decision in September a very close call. Over the shorter
term, we expect EUR/USD probably to trade out a 1.14-1.15 range and take its cue from the

data.
Europe today will see its first look at second quarter GDP prints and also indications of July
inflation. Weak growth (the eurozone is expected at 0.2% quarter-on-quarter) probably will not
stand in the way of an ECB hike in September, which is more than 90% priced. An uptick in July
inflation data today and high oil prices should keep short-dated euro rates supported.
Chris Turner

### Page 3

THINK economic and financial analysis
Article | 30 July 2026 3
GBP: Focus on a 7-2 BoE hold today
The Bank of England is widely expected to keep rates on hold at 3.75% today. A 7-2 vote for
unchanged rates is consensus and new inflation forecasts should support the doves. The BoE is
probably not under as much pressure as the Fed to hike and as tightening expectations fade
later this year, sterling should gradually weaken. EUR/GBP could make a run at 0.8600/8610

today.
Chris Turner
CEE: Growth data and Fed signals keep FX in focus
Today brings the first hard CEE data of the week, with flash 2Q GDP estimates from the Czech
Republic and Hungary. We expect Czech GDP at 0.5% QoQ and 2.1% YoY, versus 0.2% QoQ and
2.2% YoY previously, signalling stronger sequential momentum despite slightly softer annual
growth. In Hungary, we expect GDP to rise to 1.2% QoQ and 2.4% YoY from 0.8% QoQ and
1.7% YoY, pointing to stronger activity than earlier estimates implied and supporting a more

constructive growth outlook.
Yesterday’s Fed meeting delivered mixed signals for EM: a weaker US dollar on the one hand,
but a sell-off at the long end of the curve on the other. We think the initial reaction in EM and
CEE should be stronger FX, with the softer dollar dominating for now. The rates impact is less
straightforward. Firmer FX should help anchor the front end, while the long end may face
pressure from higher long-term US rates. As in the US market, this points to a steepening bias

in CEE, especially where central banks are dovish, namely Hungary and Poland.
For now, markets can still take some relief from FX, with EUR/HUF back below 362 and EUR/PLN
below 4.320.
Frantisek Taborsky

Charts of the week: Earnings likely to trump geopolitics

Oil prices and rate expectations are likely near their peak; strong earnings upgrades are likely to drive equities higher

Source: Bloomberg, LSEG I/B/E/S, Standard Chartered

Editorial 
Geopolitics, policy and earnings 
Strategy summary:  The Houthis’ blockade of the Red Sea 
following the resumption of US -Iran hostilities has driven a 
sharp rebound in energy prices and bond yields. We expect US 
electoral constraints and Iranian restraint to eventually limit 
escalation in the conflict. Agai nst this backdrop, central banks 
are likely to hold rates this month as they assess the impact of 
any temporary spike in energy prices on the inflation outlook.  
Besides the spike in bond yields, equity markets will have to 
contend with lofty earnings expectations as the reporting 
season picks up. We expect strong AI investment -driven 
earnings beats, especially in the US and Asia, to drive upgrades 
to profit estimates. This should support continued equity market 
outperformance in the coming months. In fixed income, 
inflation-protected bonds appear particularly attractive, 
especially in the US, where the inflation -adjusted yield has 
surged above 2.4%, close to the highest since 2008.  
Mideast conflict limited by US electoral constraints, Iran 
restraint: The re-escalation of tensions, with Yemen’s Houthis 
joining the conflict, has driven European gas prices back to 
March peaks, but US gasoline prices remain well below the 
latest peaks. Notably, Iran has avoided attacking Saudi Arabia 
and UAE in the latest  escalation, signalling restraint. While 
there is a window for further near-term escalation, any rebound 
in US gasoline prices closer to May’s peaks in the run -up to 
November’s mid -term elections is likely to put pressure on 
President Trump to resume negot iations. Thus, we expect US 
crude oil (WTI) to trade largely in the USD 70 -90/bbl range in 
the next few months, limiting the economic impact.    
Central banks to hold rates this month.  We expect major 
central banks to hold rates this month as they assess the 
inflationary impact of the latest Middle East escalation. This was 
apparent from this week’s ECB decision to hold its deposit rate 
at 2.25%, while it kept the option open for another rate hike later 
this year. Meanwhile, the softer -than-expected US inflation 
report for June confirmed our view that inflation peaked in Q2. 
We expect the Fed to stay on hold this year as long as gasoline 
prices remain subdued, given slowing wage growth and cooling 
shelter and services inflation. As such, the 2-5-year segment of 
the bond yield curve looks increasingly attractive, especially if 
rate expectations peak with oil prices in the next few weeks.  
ECB, BoJ under rising pressure to hike. The ECB, being an 
inflation-targeting central bank, is under growing pressure to 
hike rates in the coming months to counter any energy -price 
driven inflation if European natural gas prices stay elevated. We 
raise the probability of another 25bps rate hike later this year, 
with the timing dependent on the status of the US -Iran conflict 
and the pass-through of energy prices onto inflation and wage 
growth. The BoE is likely to be constrained from hiking rates, 
though, due to weakening domestic growth outlook. Meanwhile, 
the BoJ remains behind the curve as a weaker currency pours 
fuel on domestic wage -driven inflation. There is rising chance 
the BoJ hikes twice this year, if only to support the JPY. 
Equities under rates, earnings scanner. Equities slumped on 
Thursday weighed by a Big Tech earnings report, surging crude 
oil prices (WTI crossed USD 90/bbl and Brent rose above USD 
100/bbl) and the US 10 -year government bond yield rising 
above 4.7%. The 10-year yield has gained almost 35bps since 
29 June. We believe a 25bps rise in bond yields, which serves 
as the discount rate for valuing equities, can impact global 
equities by around 3-4%, so investors should remain disciplined 
as rate expectations shift. However, projected earnings growth 
of 15 -20% in 2026 an d 2027 should provide a meaningful 
cushion against rate-driven valuation pressure. 
US earnings key support for equity outperformance . Big 
Tech is likely to show strong results and clearer AI monetisation 
in Q2. The tech sector has driven over half of global equity EPS 
upgrades since end-February, while AI adoption is still early in 
a multi-year cycle. We remain overweight US technology and 
communication services, and favour regions with stronger 
earnings visibility, particularly the US and Asia ex-Japan.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 24 July 2026

Top client questions 
  What do you expect from US Big Tech earnings next week? 
Our view: We expect US Big Tech companies to deliver solid Q2 
earnings while demonstrating further evidence  of AI 
monetisation. We are Overweight the US technology and 
communication services sectors. 
Rationale: The US technology sector continues to be a primary 
driver of S&P500 earnings, with the highest projected earnings 
growth over the next 12 months. It is expected to see earnings 
growth of 65.6% y/y in Q2 and 57% in 2026, driven by AI demand. 
Besides strong earnings growth, we expect US Big Tech companies 
to demonstrate that elevated AI capex is translating into stronger 
monetisation. US Census Bureau data indicates that AI adoption 
has already reached 20 -25% across US industries. This rapid 
uptake signals strong enterprise acceptance and gives us greater 
confidence that AI usage remains in the early stages of a multi-year 
growth cycle. The emergence of cost-effective and high-performing 
Chinese AI models has raised some concerns about AI 
commoditisation, which  can reduce the returns on AI capex. 
However, we believe that as AI resources become more efficient to 
use, total consumption will rise (Jevons paradox). To gain exposure, 
we are Overweight the US technology and communication services 
sectors, with the latter being dominated by large internet platforms. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist 
 What is your Q2 earnings outlook for the Japan and Europe 
financial sectors? 
Our view: We expect a constructive earnings season for Japan 
and Europe ex-UK banks and remain Overweight both sectors. 
Rationale: The earnings backdrop for Japan banks is supported by 
the BoJ’s two 25bps rate hikes in December 2025 and Jun e 2026, 
which are feeding through via  lagged loan repricing  and 
expanding domestic net interest margins . Notably, the 
regulatory easing  of Japan banks’ exposure limits  for individual 
corporate groups grants megabanks greater flexibility to underwrite 
large-scale M&A and AI data centre transactions. While the near -
term earnings benefit is incremental, the medium -term implications 
are more meaningful, with greater flexibility to support loan growth.  
Europe banks  are also enter ing the  Q2 earnings season with a 
constructive set of catalysts. Net interest income is benefiting from a 
higher-for-longer rate path, and fee income is supported by resilient 
investment banking activity and a stabilising wealth management 
segment. Meanwhile, credit quality remains robust , with the 
European Banking Authority's June 2026 Risk Assessment showing 
non-performing loan ratios sitting at historic lows. Sustained capital 
distributions via dividends and share buybacks continue to be a key 
catalyst and represent a compelling risk-adjusted return opportunity. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

The technology sector is expected to continue 
leading the US market in earnings growth over the 
next 12 months

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 24 July 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What might be the implications of next week’s  Fed, BoJ 
and BoE policy meetings for bond and FX markets? 
Our view: We expect the Fed, BoJ and BoE to keep policy rates 
unchanged. Guidance will be key: i) a neutral Fed should limit 
further gains in US bond yields and USD, ii) BoJ is expected to 
keep the door open to further policy normalisation; and iii) the 
BoE is likely to retain a cautious inflation bias. 
Rationale: We expect the Fed to hold rates at 3.5-3.75%. Softer 
June US consumer inflation  supports a hold, although sustained 
strength in WTI oil prices could reinforce upside inflation risks. 
A neutral message w ill likely cap short -term gains in government 
bond yields and reduce support for the USD, while renewed 
emphasis on inflation risks would lift yields elevated. The USD Index 
(DXY) is likely to remain rangebound between 100.3 and 102.  
The BoJ is likely to hold its policy rate at 1.00% next week , but 
the weak JPY and imported inflation should preserve the possibility 
of further normalisation. Reports suggest some policymakers see 
scope for faster policy normalisation if JPY weakness, higher fuel 
costs and quicker cost pass -through push inflation above 
projections. Combined with our expectation that the Fed  will remain 
on hold, this should eventually allow USD/JPY to ease. Technically, 
we believe the USD/JPY correction is likely to be gradual rather than 
immediate. FX intervention and higher pension fund allocations 
to domestic assets may slow JPY depreciation, but a sustained 
reversal will require a narrower US-Japan interest rate differential. A 
sustained move below 160 will bring the 155-158 range into focus. 
We expect another hold by BoE on 30 July, following June’s 7-2 vote 
to hold rates at 3.75%. Persistent energy-driven inflation could keep 
UK Gilt yields elevated and support GBP, although weak activity and 
fiscal uncertainty may limit gains. GBP/USD is likely to stay 
rangebound between 1.3200 and 1.3500.   
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
 Will the PBoC implement monetary easing measures , 
given China’s weak Q2 GDP? Will this affect the CNH outlook? 
Our view: A reserve requirement ratio (RRR) cut remains 
possible, but any resulting CNH depreciation should be limited.  
Rationale: China’s economy grew 4.7% in H1, in line with the 
government’s annual growth target, although Q2 GDP moderated to 
4.3% y/y from 5.0%  in Q1 . Industrial production and exports 
remained resilient . Overall macroeconomic conditions do not 
suggest a urgent need for broad-based monetary easing. An RRR 
cut could weigh on the CNH initially by reinforcing concerns over 
wider policy divergence with the US. However, such a cut primarily 
releases banking system liquidity and does not necessarily signal 
a shift towards aggressive monetary easing. We therefore expect 
any CNH weakness to remain  measured, supported by China’s 
external balance and policymakers’ preference for exchange -rate 
stability. USD/CNH has near term support at 6.75. 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

between 100.3 and 102

Document 308: Global Strategy Paper_ Balancing Innovation and Inflation in Portfolios

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Paper_ Balancing Innovation and Inflation in Portfolios.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-07-10
Pages: 35
Status: ok

### Page 1

GLOBAL STRATEGY PAPER
NO. 77
PORTFOLIO STRATEGY RESEARCH | 10 July 2026 | 5:00 PM BST
Balancing Innovation and Inflation in Portfolios
Global financial assets have performed strongly in the last 3 years after the large•
drawdown in balanced portfolios in 2022. Equities have performed particularly
strongly, with long-term ex post equity risk premia nearing the Golden 20s and 50s
and the AI boom boosting Tech stocks. As a result, the 'World Portfolio' has drifted

towards US assets, equities and the Tech sector in recent years.
Structural cycles linked to innovation or inflation have historically often driven•
prolonged trends in performance across assets, regions and sectors and, as a result,
the composition of the 'World Portfolio' has materially drifted – increasing risk in the
case of structural breaks. The current AI capex boom increases the risk that falling
profitability for mega-cap Tech stocks materially drags on equity returns before

benefits from AI adoption show up.
And higher inflation volatility and fiscal risks are also creating headwinds for•
balanced portfolios, with less of a buffer fr om bonds and more risk of rate shocks.
Based on our macro-based return forecasting framework, in all but an optimistic
'Goldilocks + AI boom' scenario, expected long-term equity returns are below the
long-run average, pointing to lower equity allocations.
The composition of market value-weighted global multi-asset benchmarks drifts•

due to asset supply and relative performance and, as a result, it is often backward-
looking. Regularly rebalanced 'regime-neutral' portfolios outperformed the 'World
Portfolio' over the long run and currently point to lower weights in equities and Tech
and higher allocations to real assets.
However, during strong equity bull markets, especially if they are driven by the Tech•
sector, it was difficult t o lean against momentum. In the short term, being

underinvested can be costly, with equities usually performing well up until the bull
market peak. Investors need to keep exposure to innovation, protect against
inflation and improve risk mitigation – we recommend five strategies to stay invested
while balancing innovation and inflation in global multi-asset portfolios.
Christian Mueller-Glissmann, CFA
+44(20)7774-1714
christian.mueller-glissmann@gs.com
Goldman Sachs International
Andrea Ferrario

Goldman Sachs International

### Page 2

Summary

Global financial assets have performed strongly in the last 3 years after the large n
drawdown in balanced portfolios in 2022. Our ‘World Portfolio’ or a US 60/40 
portfolio delivered unusually strong real returns since the GFC, comparable to the 
Golden 20s and 50s. 
Ex post equity risk premia have also been particularly high and were boosted by n
strong growth in the Tech sector, in particular in the US and, more recently, in Asia. 
As a result, the composition of the ‘World Portfolio’ has drifted towards US assets 
(also supported by a stronger Dollar since the GFC), equities and the Tech sector. 
Innovation and inflation have been among the most important drivers of structural n
cycles. Those structural cycles have historically driven prolonged trends in 
performance across assets, regions and sectors and, as a result, major drifts in the 
composition of the ‘World Portfolio’. During periods of innovation, equities tend to 
perform well, particularly growth stocks and the Tech sector, and balanced 
portfolios deliver the strongest Sharpe ratios. However, balanced portfolios often 
eventually become imbalanced. 
The current AI capex boom increases the risk that falling profitability for mega-cap n
Tech stocks materially drags on equity returns before benefits from AI adoption 
show up. Higher inflation volatility and fiscal risks also create headwinds for 
balanced portfolios with less of a buffer from bonds and risk of rate shocks. Based 
on our long-term return forecasting framework, in all but a very bullish ‘Goldilocks + 
AI boom’ scenario, expected long-term equity returns are below the long-run 
average.  
The composition of market value-weighted global multi-asset portfolios drifts due n
to asset supply and relative performance and, as a result, is often backward-looking. 
Optimal portfolios, in contrast, should be conditioned on future macro regimes. 
Regularly rebalanced ‘regime-neutral’ portfolios outperformed the ‘World Portfolio’ n
over the long run and currently point to lower weights in equities and Tech and 
higher allocations to real assets. However, during strong equity bull markets, 
especially if they are driven by the Tech sector, it was difficult to lean against 
momentum. 
However, even though valuations are high and uncertainty around both innovation n
and inflation remains high, it remains difficult to lean against the current momentum 
– time in the market competes with timing the market. We find that equities deliver 
some of their strongest returns in the final years of a bull market, often led by the 
sector that outperformed in the preceding years. 
Investors need to keep exposure to innovation, protect against inflation and improve n
risk mitigation in portfolios. We recommend five strategies that help investors stay 
invested and achieve those objectives: (1) selective real asset allocations, (2) 
opportunities in factors and style diversification, (3) regional diversification 
opportunities across assets, (4) option strategies such as long-dated calls and (5) 
selective alternatives that are uncorrelated with the ‘World Portfolio’. 
10 July 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Strategy Paper

### Page 4

Imbalanced portfolios — Tech stock momentum becomes key driver

Bull market in financial assets has resumed 
In the last 3 years, financial assets recovered strongly after the large drawdown in 
balanced portfolios (‘Balanced Bear’) in 2022. In Global Strategy Paper: Investing in 
Everything, Everywhere, All at Once, we introduced a ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark 
tracking roughly US$300tn of global financial assets. Over the last 3 years, the returns of 
this ‘World Portfolio’ have been among the strongest since WW2 (Exhibit 7). Similarly, a 
US 60/40 portfolio, which we can track back to 1900, has seldom delivered stronger 
returns outside of the Golden 20s, 50s, the early 1980s and the late 1990s.

Exhibit 7: Balanced portfolios have delivered strong returns in recent years

### Page 5

Balanced portfolios, especially in the US, are closing in on the longest and strongest 
periods of long-run performance on record – the last decade’s real returns for a US 
60/40 portfolio are close to 8%, or nearly twice the long-run average. There have only 
been similarly strong periods of real returns during the Golden 20s and 50s, and the 
1990s. On the flipside, there were several periods of unusually poor real returns, such as 
around WW1 and WW2, as well as the 1970s and the 2000s Tech Bubble Burst. The 
‘World Portfolio’ performed less strongly in real terms, as non-US assets generally 
underperformed US assets, in part due to a strong Dollar. 
Our ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark is now roughly half in US$ assets and half in 
non-US$ assets. US$ assets have been a dominant driver of growth in financial assets 
post GFC – their proportion has steadily increased after declining during the 2000s 
(Exhibit 9). Post GFC, the US$ bond weight picked up owing to higher yields relative to 
the Rest of the World, given a better post-GFC recovery, the Fed’s avoidance of negative 
rates, and a strong Dollar. Since the GFC, US equities have materially outperformed 
non-US markets, at least until recently - as a result, the MSCI AC World equity 
benchmark currently has a c.63% US weight.

High equity risk premia boost equity weight in the ‘World Portfolio’ 
Ex post equity risk premia have been very high and were boosted further by poor 
bond returns since the COVID-19 crisis (Exhibit 10). The last decade’s ex post equity 
risk premia are well above the long-run average of 4.5-5%. In fact, the annualised US 
equity vs. bond performance has been nearly three times the long-term average. The 
only prolonged periods of similarly high equity risk premia were during the Golden 20s 
and 50s. Equities also performed well during both the early 1980s and the late 1990s, 
but the outperformance vs. bonds was less pronounced. Poor bond returns in recent 
years, due to very low starting yields after the COVID-19 crisis and higher inflation and 
fiscal risks since then, have added to ex post equity risk premia.

Exhibit 9: US assets have become more dominant in the World Portfolio again

### Page 6

As a result, the equity weight in the ‘World Portfolio’ is nearing Tech Bubble peaks, 
especially when private markets are included (Exhibit 11). In the 2000s, the equity 
weight trended down when the Tech Bubble burst and again following the GFC. However, 
since the GFC, strong performance has boosted the equity weight once again. Although 
equities have since regained some ground, their share remains below late-1990s levels, 
which were further boosted by the IPO boom of that period; in recent years, buybacks 
have dominated instead. However, when including private markets, which contain 
several large Tech unicorns that might IPO in the coming years, the ‘World Portfolio’ 
weight in equities is closing in on the Tech Bubble peak.

### Page 7

Technology dominance has driven equity benchmark concentration 
Strong equity performance has been boosted by the Technology sector, which has 
become increasingly dominant within equities (Exhibit 12). Structural cycles linked to 
the Technology sector have historically often been a key driver of the equity weight, 
notably during the PC boom of the 1960s, the 1990s Internet bubble, and the rise of the 
Magnificent 7, or ‘hyperscalers’, since the GFC. Different sectors have also regularly 
dominated equity performance historically – Commodities sectors were a dominant 
driver during the 1950s, the 1970s and around the GFC, while Financials outperformed 
in the 2000s, boosted by increased financialisation of the global economy.

Since the GFC, the global TMT sector (combining Technology and Communication 
Services) has gained material share in the global equity benchmark. More recently 
optimism around AI has supported Tech sector returns and there have been more

Exhibit 12: Tech sector outperformance has been unusually strong and prolonged

### Page 8

capital raises and IPOs – the current weight in the US equity benchmark is already above 
the Tech Bubble peak (Exhibit 13). However, in contrast to the Tech Bubble, the Tech 
sector weight has increased over multiple years, indicating that it has been more closely 
linked to fundamentals. Financials, which lost ground after the GFC, have also started to 
recover since the COVID-19 crisis, while Commodity and Defensive sectors such as 
Healthcare and Utilities have lost share. 
A consequence of the growing Technology sector has been a higher concentration in 
a few large cap stocks, which have contributed significantly to strong index returns 
(Exhibit 14). The same was true during the Tech Bubble, but earnings are now much 
more concentrated. Still, depending on the degree of correlation among the constituent 
companies and with the index itself, greater index concentration may reduce 
diversification benefits and increase overall risk. More recently, Tech dominance has also 
increased in Asia, with narrow performance led by AI capex beneficiaries, especially 
semiconductors (Exhibit 15). While the ‘hyperscalers’ in the US have historically been less 
correlated and less cyclical, with strong balance sheets and cash flows generation1, 
semiconductor companies in North Asia are highly correlated and cyclical businesses. 
1 In fact Kritzman and Turkington (2026) argue that the US stock market has not become riskier as it has 
become more concentrated, in part as larger companies are more diversified and resilient - they show that since 
1926 their increased weights are offset by their lower volatility compared to small stocks.

### Page 9

Innovation vs. Inflation as structural drivers of balanced portfolios

Structural cycles and balanced portfolio performance 
Innovation and inflation have among been the most important drivers of trends 
across asset classes, regions and sectors. Slow-moving trends in growth, inflation and 
policy can result in structural cycles – these are often linked to demographics and social 
attitudes, technological developments and productivity trends, as well as policy choices 
and regimes. 60/40 portfolios delivered the highest returns during periods of successful 
innovation with anchored inflation and rising productivity growth, such as the Golden 
20s and 50s, the 1990s and the post-GFC period. On the flip side, prolonged periods of 
high inflation, often due to wars, resulted in ‘lost decades’ (Exhibit 16).

Balanced portfolios perform best in a structural ‘Goldilocks’ scenario with low 
inflation and good growth – as Exhibit 17 shows, the sweet spot occurs when the 
economy is neither too hot nor too cold. With low and anchored inflation, bonds tend to 
act as a buffer for equities during growth shocks, resulting in better risk-adjusted returns 
for US 60/40 portfolios. On average, returns declined with lower trend growth and real 
returns were poor during stagflationary episodes. A favourable structural 
growth/inflation mix also tends to boost equity risk premia.

### Page 10

Innovation and rising productivity growth have often resulted in prolonged periods 
with a favourable structural growth/inflation mix (Exhibit 18). The early 20th century 
was defined by the Internal Combustion Engine and Electrification, enabling the rise of 
mass production, urbanisation, and global logistics, resulting in the Golden 20s. The 
mid-century invention of the Transistor and the Microprocessor catalysed the 
computing revolution, automating clerical work and laying the groundwork for the 
Internet – leading to the Golden 50s. Since the 1990s, globally integrated trade and 
supply chains have become tailwinds for manufacturing in developed markets. Since the 
2000s, the internet has led to the rise of platform companies, or ‘hyperscalers’, 
leveraging network effects to scale at near-zero marginal cost. Our economists expect AI 
to meaningfully boost productivity growth over the next decade through task 
automation in knowledge industries.

Innovation and valuation frustration 
During such periods of innovation with a favourable growth/inflation mix, both 
equities and bonds tend to become more expensive (Exhibit 19). As we first 
highlighted in our ‘Balanced Bear’ research in 2021, this increases vulnerability to 
stagflationary shocks – the post COVID-19 reopening surge in inflation eventually 
triggered one of the largest drawdowns for 60/40 portfolios on record. Since then, 
cyclically-adjusted equity valuations have recovered and are back near all-time highs. 
However, due to continued inflation volatility, more recently linked to the Middle East 
war, and fiscal concerns, global bond yields have settled near their long-run averages. 
This points to lower prospective equity risk premia.

### Page 11

Historically, S&P 500 Shiller P/Es often increased before productivity growth picked 
up, in part because front-loaded investments shifted earnings above their 
long-term trend. This can result in a boom/bust cycle in the event of concerns about 
overinvestment or insufficient productivity benefits from the technology. During Tech 
booms, there is often a pick-up in competition and new disruptive technologies, which 
can weigh on profitability and increase the risk of excess capacity. As a result, there is a 
risk of bubbles similar to those seen in the late 1990s – this has again become a key 
concern as Shiller P/Es have picked up materially since the GFC while productivity growth 
has remained subdued.

### Page 12

From spending to earning — capex vs. productivity bene fits 
An important difference between the current AI boom and the 2000 Tech Bubble 
has been high corporate profitability. Higher ROEs help explain higher S&P 500 Shiller 
P/Es since the 1990s - they have been boosted by rising profit margins, extending a 
trend that started in the early 1980s. Post GFC, US corporate profitability increased 
further owing to the fast-growing US Tech sector, falling interest costs, low labour cost 
inflation, tax cuts and share buybacks. The S&P 500 trailing 4-quarter ROE reached a 
record high of 22% in Q1 2026, surpassing the previous high reached in 2021.

While US GDP growth was relatively low post GFC, the corporate sector materially 
outperformed the broader economy in the US (Exhibit 22). Similarly, the global Tech 
sector has been a major driver of earnings growth for global equities – in fact, since the 
GFC, earnings growth for the MSCI World excluding Tech has been much lower and 
below long-run averages. As our US strategy team has shown, the rising share of mega 
cap Tech stocks such as the ‘hyperscalers’, with their capital-light business models and 
strong revenue growth, has been a key driver of this trend (Exhibit 23).

### Page 13

However, the ‘hyperscalers’ have now materially increased their AI capex spending. 
As we wrote in Global Strategy Paper: The Post Modern Cycle – Navigating the Capex 
Boom, this has boosted profitability for capex beneficiaries such as semiconductor 
companies.  The ‘hyperscalers’ are now on track to allocate all of their cash flows from 
operations to capex (Exhibit 24). As a result the group has sharply reduced share 
repurchases and begun raising additional capital via equity and debt issuance. As many 
of the beneficiaries, such as semiconductor companies, are highly cyclical, the risk from 
reductions in AI capex has increased. Our US strategy team remains optimistic about 
near-term momentum in AI capex. However, AI capex growth is likely to slow down 
eventually due to efficiency gains, competition and capital constraints.

And as our US strategy team highlighted, the outlook for profitability among the 
hyperscalers is becoming more challenging. They are becoming increasingly 
asset-heavy businesses. AI capex has led to a decline in asset turnover (sales/assets), 
which analysts expect will continue in 2027 (Exhibit 25). Consensus estimates also 
suggest that each of the mega-cap Tech stocks will experience declining ROEs from next 
year, by an average of 7 pp (Exhibit 26). The hyperscalers and the broader AI ecosystem 
will have to demonstrate that they can monetise AI applications – a process that may 
take longer, with profitability potentially declining first. 
As our economists wrote, while ICT investment began rising sharply in the early 
1980s, industry-level data show that its impact on productivity followed a J-curve: 
a modest drag on measured productivity growth in the first four years, with clear gains 
emerging only after eight years and peaking in year twelve. Having said that, they expect 
AI to have a quicker impact on productivity because costs are falling much faster for AI 
models than they did for ICT equipment, and network effects are less vital to realising 
productivity gains. While AI adoption should support broader corporate profitability in 
the long run, it may also cause greater disruption to existing business models on the way, 
particularly in software.

Risks from inflation for valuations 
While innovation creates upside for balanced portfolios, inflation can drive 
downside risks. Exhibit 27 shows that low and anchored inflation, i.e., US CPI inflation 
close to or slightly below 2%, has historically anchored 60/40 yields lower, while either 
very high or very low inflation has weighed on valuations. As we wrote before, inflation 
volatility and fiscal spending are likely to be higher due to a combination of adverse 
trends: (1) Deglobalisation, both economically and geopolitically, (2) Decarbonisation, with 
the risk of supply shocks and rising costs associated with climate change, and (3) 
Demographics, such as lower population growth, higher dependency ratios and income 
inequality. In addition, the current AI capex boom may drive further inflationary 
pressures - as our economists recently highlighted, the spike in memory and chip prices 
has boosted core PCE, albeit partly due to measurement issues.

Long-dated bond yields tend to increase with rising nominal growth, even if that

### Page 15

growth is solely due to inflation - the 10-year US nominal GDP growth, i.e. real growth 
+ inflation, has explained nearly 90% of the variation in US 10-year yields since WW2 
(Exhibit 28). Other drivers, such as monetary and fiscal policy regimes, inflation volatility 
and expectations for productivity growth, can also influence long-dated bond yields. As 
our rates team has highlighted, the AI boom could increase neutral rates by raising the 
expected return on capital, leading to lower savings and higher investments. 
The response of equity valuations to inflation is less symmetric (Exhibit 29). A good 
environment for equities has been range-bound inflation between 1% and 3%, which 
has mostly been the case since the late 1990s. High and rising inflation (>3%), as seen 
during the 1970s and in 2022, weighed materially on equity valuations to concerns 
about profit margins and rising discount rates. However, too low inflation increases 
deflation fears, pushing up equity risk premia as the risk of profit stagnation and 
defaults increases. This was the case during the Great Depression of the 1930s, as well 
as in Europe and Japan during the 1990s and post-GFC periods, respectively.

Updating our structural cycle scenarios — between AI boom and bust  
The outlook for balanced portfolios in the coming years is closely linked to the AI 
boom and risks from inflation. We derive new scenarios using our macro-based 
long-term return framework from Global Strategy Paper: The Strategic Balanced Bear 
(Exhibit 30): (1) Goldilocks + AI boom, with continued ROE expansion and strong GDP 
growth, (2) Goldilocks, but with stable ROE, (3) Reflation, with strong nominal growth 
but lower ROE and (4) Goldilocks + AI bust, in which ROE declines and GDP growth 
weakens, (5) Stagnation, with weaker growth but ROE somewhat in-line with its 5-year 
average and (6) stagflation, similar to the 1970s. 
In all scenarios except ‘Goldilocks + AI boom’, equity returns are below the long-run 
average. Equity risk premia for both the ‘normal’ Goldilocks backdrop and for Reflation 
are near the long-run average – this still suggests equity allocations that are closer to the 
long-run average optimal levels of 50-60%. However, for the more negative scenarios 
equity returns and equity risk premia are much worse, pointing to lower equity 
allocations going forward. But bond returns are also somewhat below average in most 
scenarios and unlikely to provide much a large buffer in those adverse scenarios, 
especially those with inflation risk.

### Page 17

The World (Portfolio) is not enough — investigating regime-neutral portfolios

Market-value weighted benchmarks are seldom optimal 
As the weights of asset classes, regions and sectors within market value-weighted 
benchmarks drift as a function of past performance and asset supply, they tend to 
be backward-looking. Optimal portfolios, in contrast, vary as a function of future 
macro regimes. To compare the ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark with what would have been 
mean-variance-efficient, we split it into 5 big building blocks (US and non-US equities, 
US and non-US bonds, and Gold) and create ex-post optimal portfolios. Unsurprisingly, 
over shorter horizons, for example 1-3 years, business cycle swings and bear markets 
drove large market timing opportunities (in hindsight) and ex-post optimal Sharpe ratios 
are much higher (Exhibit 31).

Even over 10-year rolling horizons, the ex-post optimal portfolio mix shifted 
notably over time (Exhibit 32). As we discuss in Global Strategy Paper: The Strategic 
Balanced Bear, structural cycles, i.e. trends in growth, inflation and policy, impact 
optimal multi-asset portfolios for prolonged periods of time. During the Golden 50s, the 
1990s and the post-GFC period, there were larger allocations to US equities due to a 
boost from technological innovations. In the 1960s and 70s, the optimal portfolio had no 
allocations to bonds. In the 1970s stagflation Gold dominated the optimal portfolio and 
similarly played a more important role during the GFC and in recent years. After the GFC 
and before the COVID-19 crisis, a US risk parity portfolio (30-40% equities, 60-70% 
bonds) had the highest risk-adjusted returns. Since the COVID-19 crisis, the optimal US 
equity weight has increased, and more recently so has the optimal allocation to Gold. 
The ex-post optimal portfolio over the last decade was roughly 50% US equities and 
50% Gold.

Exhibit 31: The World Portfolio materially lags the most efficient portfolio, even over longer 
horizons

### Page 18

Regime-neutral portfolios tend to outperform in the medium-term 
This raises the question of how to construct a portfolio that performs robustly across 
macro regimes. This is important to assess and manage drifts in market value-weighted 
benchmark weights and realistically outperform the ‘World Portfolio’. We construct 4 
simple, regularly rebalanced portfolios that aim to be ‘regime-neutral’. We define a 
‘regime-neutral’ portfolio as one whose weights do not rely on any single macro regime 
so that it performs well through cycle and is identifiable ex ante. In practice, we 
approximate this concept using different approaches: 
The average of the 10-year rolling ex-post optimal portfolio weights since 1950 1.
(from Exhibit 32). This approach aims to average out variation in optimal weights due 
to different macro regimes to create a ‘neutral’ starting point.2 It incorporates 
historically changing risk/rewards and correlations across assets. 
The average of the historical weights of the ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark since 2.
1950, assuming they represent the long-run equilibrium. Average asset weights since 
1950 have been 25% US equity, 24% non-US equity, 30% US bonds, 18% non-US 
bonds and 4% Gold. 
A risk-parity strategy, which allocates to assets based on their risk to reduce risk 3.
concentration towards equities that is common in balanced portfolios. There is no 
need for return or correlation assumptions. We use a simple approach that weights 
assets based on the inverse of their volatility of monthly returns since 1950. 
Naïve 1/N weights, i.e. equal weights, as a robustness check to address the 4.
concerns about out-of-sample performance of optimal portfolios raised by 
DeMiguel et.al. (2009). 
2 This is similar to the idea of a constructing a resampled efficient frontier from Michaud (1998), who uses 
Monte Carlo resamples of the inputs reduce estimation error rather than averaging optimal portfolio weights 
across historical windows.

Exhibit 32: Over 10-year rolling horizons, the optimal portfolio shifted notably over time, 
deviating materially from the World Portfolio benchmark 
10-year rolling weight of ex-post highest Sharpe ratio portfolio (data since 1950)

### Page 19

All regularly rebalanced ‘regime-neutral’ approaches outperformed the ‘World 
Portfolio’ on a risk-adjusted basis since 1950 (Exhibit 33). They mostly had better hit 
ratios of generating attractive real returns over the long run while reducing the risk of 
negative real returns. Compared to the current ‘World Portfolio’ weights, the 
‘regime-neutral’ portfolios generally point to somewhat smaller weights in equities (and 
US equities in particular), larger weights to Gold and smaller weights to non-US bonds. 
The risk-parity strategy had the lowest Sharpe ratio, while the one based on 10-year 
rolling average optimal weights offered the highest Sharpe ratios. While the 
‘regime-neutral’ portfolios are estimated ex post, the persistent improvement observed 
over long horizons suggests that regime diversification, rather than timing, is the key 
driver. Even the naïve 1/N strategy outperformed the ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark, 
indicating that the results are not solely dependent on optimisation.

Leaning against momentum can be costly in the short-term 
While the benefits of regime diversification accrue over the long-run, benchmark 
drift can dominate relative performance in the short-term. Comparing relative 
performance on a risk-neutral basis shows prolonged periods (usually during strong 
equity bull markets) when the different ‘regime-neutral’ portfolios underperformed 
(Exhibit 34). In those periods benchmark drifts can exacerbate positive momentum. For 
example, during the Tech Bubble, all ‘regime-neutral’ portfolios struggled to keep up 
with the ‘World Portfolio’, which had a growing equity weight. This was particularly true 
for the risk parity-based and equal-weight portfolios due to their lower equity weight. 
The same was true during the Golden 1950s and 1960s. This illustrates the difficulty of 
leaning against benchmark drifts in equities, especially during strong bull markets. 
Approaches to better time when to lean against large benchmark drifts could be based 
on ‘regime-based’ asset allocation signals (see Global Strategy Paper: The Dynamic 
Balanced Bear) or momentum-based signals and volatility targeting overlays (see Global 
Strategy Paper: Balanced Bear Despair - Part 4).

### Page 20

Leaning against sector drifts within equity benchmarks is especially difficult. As 
shown in Exhibit 35, over 10-year rolling horizons the ex-post optimal sector mix within 
a US equity benchmark shifted notably over time. For example during Technology 
booms like the 1950s, late 1990s and more recently, there have been material tilts 
towards the Technology sector. The ‘regime-neutral’ Technology sector weight (c.18%) is 
well below current levels within US equities (c.47%). During periods of strong 
momentum in the Technology sector, it can be costly to lean against benchmark drifts 
and rebalance back to ‘regime-neutral’ weights. With historical high volatility of the 
Technology sector, especially during booms, investors need to balance near-term 
tracking error risk from reducing the Technology sector weight with potential long-term 
improvements in the risk/reward.

### Page 21

Benefits from broadening real asset allocations 
The vulnerability of balanced equity/bond portfolios to higher inflation, especially 
stagflation regimes, points to benefits from larger allocations to real assets. ‘Real 
assets’ also provided diversification in Tech-led equity drawdowns. Due to size and 
liquidity constraints we included only Gold in our ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark. Over the 
long run, Gold had a low Sharpe ratio but occasionally, like in recent years or the 1970s 
stagflation, it delivered very strong returns and diversification for balanced equity/bond 
portfolios. But Gold is an incomplete inflation hedge; while it protected purchasing 
power over the long run, it had a mixed relationship with inflation over shorter horizons – 
in recent years it has performed well mainly due to concerns about currency 
debasement and institutional credibility. 
Adding a broader set of real assets can better diversify different sources of 
inflation. If inflation is demand-driven, companies with pricing power and cyclical 
commodities benefit, while cost-push inflation due to energy supply shocks favours 
related commodities. While real asset stocks (infrastructure/real estate/energy) cannot 
provide an imminent inflation hedge they protect from inflation in the medium-term and 
have higher Sharpe ratios. We include the equal-weight index of Gold, S&P GSCI and 
different real asset stocks – as Exhibit 36 shows, compared to Gold alone this ‘Real asset 
index’ received a larger weight in a ‘regime-neutral’ portfolio since 1950. While the 
World Portfolio is currently very exposed to innovation, it might not have enough 
protection from inflation, pointing to higher allocations to real assets.

The impact of FX and the investor currency 
As US weights in global equity and bond benchmarks have risen, the Dollar has become a more important 
driver of risk-adjusted returns for non-US investors (Exhibit 37). The impact of FX risk in portfolios depends 
on foreign asset exposure, home-currency volatility relative to foreign-currency exposure, and correlations 
10 July 2026   21
Goldman Sachs Global Strategy Paper

### Page 22

with equities. The Dollar was a drag for non-US investors in the early 1990s and into the GFC, but has 
generally boosted Sharpe ratios since then, supported by its uptrend and negative correlation with risky 
assets. This was especially true for investors with cyclical currencies such as the Australian Dollar, Swedish 
Krona and Canadian Dollar, while investors with safe-haven currencies such as the Swiss Franc and 
Japanese Yen generally saw lower Sharpe ratios up until the COVID-19 crisis.

Both optimal regional equity allocations and FX hedge ratios vary by investor currency. We split global 
equities into five buckets: domestic equities, US equities hedged to domestic FX, US equities unhedged, 
World ex US equities unhedged and World ex US equities hedged. We then look at average 10-year rolling 
optimal weights (Exhibit 38):  Since 2010, large unhedged US equity weights benefited most investors, 
though less so before the Dollar strengthened. Hedge ratios and allocations also differed by currency: Swiss 
investors, for example, generally required higher FX hedge ratios and larger domestic equity allocations 
because of the Swiss Franc’s strength and safe-haven characteristics. This highlights both the benefits and 
challenges of incorporating FX in long-horizon portfolio construction.

### Page 23

Finding the right balance between Innovation and Inflation

Late cycle investing — time in the market vs. timing the market  
Even though there is a case for leaning against the drift to equities and Technology 
stocks in the medium-term, being underinvested can be costly in the short-term, 
with equities usually performing well up until the bull market peak. Avoiding large 
S&P 500 drawdowns would unsurprisingly boost performance but missing positive 
returns would have been very costly. Thus, timing the market always competes with time 
in the market. Corrections tend to be short-lived and thus difficult to time. And while 
bear markets are deeper and longer, the average profile since 1900 shows that investors 
that divested 6 months before the peak roughly lost as much as those that were 6 
months late (Exhibit 39). It is more important to avoid large losses after the start of a 
bear market.

Leading sectors also tend to continue outperforming into the bull market peak 
(Exhibit 40). As mentioned earlier, this was particularly pronounced during Tech booms 
like in the 1950s and 60s as well as the late 1990s – this makes leaning against sector 
momentum particularly costly. This is particularly true when leading sectors are being 
driven by earnings growth, as is the case today or was the case for Financials leading into 
the GFC. However, during Tech booms, price momentum can continue without 
fundamentals – during the Tech Bubble, Tech stocks continued to perform until 2000 
although profitability for the TMT sector peaked around 1996 (Exhibit 41).

Five investment ideas to balance innovation and inflation in portfolios 
Investors need to keep exposure to innovation, protect against inflation and 
improve risk mitigation within portfolios. While a growing Tech sector in benchmarks 
and portfolios makes portfolios imbalanced and more risky, leaning against a Tech boom 
is difficult. In most scenarios, long-term equity returns might be below long-run average 
but until momentum for the Tech sector more clearly moderates it is difficult to lean 
against it. We investigate five different risk mitigation strategies to help investors stay 
invested and create better balance in multi-asset portfolios. 
(1) Keeping it real — selectively increasing real asset exposure  
Allocations to real assets help create more balance in multi-asset portfolios in case 
of inflation risks, and they improve the potential for harvesting real returns. 
Historically, during some of the biggest Tech sell-offs, real assets also proved defensive – 
on average our equal-weight ‘Real Asset Index’ delivered positive returns during TMT 
sector drawdowns, particularly during the Tech Bubble burst (Exhibit 42). ‘Real Asset 
stocks’, i.e. an equal-weight index of infrastructure, real estate and energy stocks, 
generally outperformed during those Tech drawdowns and helped diversify equities.

### Page 25

With the energy shock from the Middle East war reversing and a sharp decline in oil 
prices, the benefits from different real assets are changing. During supply shocks 
linked to energy, direct commodity exposure, i.e. GSCI and oil exposures, as well as 
short-dated breakeven inflation are the main hedges, especially when central banks are 
fighting inflation. If central banks are not fighting inflation and there are concerns about 
institutional credibility and currency debasement, Gold can usually provide 
diversification. As our commodities team recently reiterated, there is still a case for 
diversification into commodities - commodities that are more demand-driven such as 
those linked to decarbonisation, EVs and renewable energy, as well as AI infrastructure 
might take over from energy. While commodities fare less well when inflation is 
normalising, real estate and infrastructure tend to perform best and would get further 
support if central banks are shifting less hawkish.

(2) Don’t lose Tech momentum, diversify it through style 
Diversifying across styles within equities can help manage risks around Tech stock 
momentum. During previous TMT sector drawdowns, we found that low volatility, high 
dividend yield stocks outperformed and there were large rotations from growth to value 
(Exhibit 44). An extreme example of the diversification benefits was the Tech Bubble 
burst when low volatility and high dividend yield stocks were up with S&P 500 down 46% 
and the TMT sector down 79%. This is in part due to them benefitting from lower bond 
yields but even during the 2022 drawdown without large decline in bond yields they 
managed to outperform materially. While cyclicals did not underperform much during 
TMT drawdowns, there was some benefit from defensive exposures. 
The ratio of momentum vs. low vol stocks nearing Tech Bubble peaks best illustrates

the risk of a reversal and need for style diversification (Exhibit 45). The recent leg in 
the momentum rally has been led by global Tech hardware stocks, especially 
semiconductors, leading the market higher. As a result, index concentration has 
increased in both the S&P 500 and MSCI Asia ex Japan. This points to potential for more 
dispersion and increasing benefits of diversification within the AI ecosystem. In addition 
with concerns about AI and disruption of capital-light business models there is potential 
for more rotation towards more capital-heavy sectors, which also fits with allocations to 
‘real assets’ – our European equity strategy team has dubbed this the HALO effect: 
Heavy Assets, Low Obsolescence, and we also have similar baskets in the US, Europe, 
Asia, EM and Japan (Exhibit 46).

(3) Don’t get lost in translation - managing US asset dominance 
We expect continued benefits from regional diversification and managing Dollar 
risk. Since last year, there has been less clear US asset outperformance (see also Global 
Strategy Paper: Magnificent Diversification). Since the start of 2025, the S&P 500 has 
lagged major global equity indices, in both local currency and US Dollar terms. 
Historically, during Tech-led equity sell-offs, US equities have on average 
underperformed other markets because they are more closely linked to innovation. FX

performance has been more mixed, but during the Tech Bubble burst the Dollar 
weakened, especially in 2002. The Swiss Franc has proven particularly defensive, 
although recent SNB interventions have changed that ‘risk-off’ characteristic. As our FX 
team recently highlighted, the Dollar and Tech sector performance have become more 
closely linked. A US equity market sell-off with clearer RoW outperformance would likely 
be a catalyst for Dollar depreciation.

In the event of a US recession, JGBs and EM bonds might provide more 
diversification within global multi-asset portfolios. The Yen recently traded at its 
weakest level versus the US Dollar in 40 years, while JGB yields are nearing multi-year 
highs. As our FX team recently showed, the USD/JPY has recently decoupled from real 
rate differentials due to rising JGB term premia (Exhibit 48). However, the Yen often 
outperforms when markets are pricing in a US recession, with Fed cuts and both equity 
and real rates lower. While near-term our markets team expects the weaker Yen/ higher 
JGB yield trend to extend, eventually it might present an attractive hedge for a Tech-led 
US equity drawdown. Similarly, EM assets, which are negatively correlated with the 
Dollar, can diversify US asset-heavy portfolios (Exhibit 49) - our EM team has also found 
that EM assets have become more resilient in ‘risk off’ episodes.

(4) Keeping all your options — long-dated calls, put spread collars, convertibles  
To improve asymmetry or increase upside convexity in a late-cycle boom, we like 
long-dated call options.  As we have shown before, long-dated call options were a very 
effective tail-risk-management strategy during the Tech Bubble. Replacing equities with 
a portfolio of 2-year 25-delta call options and IG credit can better tailor risk/reward 
profiles in late-cycle backdrops. A 4x option position to reach a starting 100-delta 
generally outperforms during strong rallies and bear markets (Exhibit 50). Investors can 
also size the number of options to match the downside tail risk of equities (6.9x) to 
unlock more upside convexity or create a defensive version (3.2x) to mitigate drawdown 
risk during bear markets. Compared with short-dated options, longer-dated calls 
benefit more from sustained equity rallies (particularly when coupled with rising 
volatility) and carry more efficiently thanks to lower theta decay and a less steep 
volatility term structure.

In bull markets, investors can also hedge equity exposures with short-dated put 
spread collars, which can help protect gains buffering smaller corrections. As we 
showed before, bullish sentiment and strong equity returns are often supported by 
favourable macro conditions, that can linger. However, the elevated risk of corrections 
makes put-spreads collars (i.e. selling calls to fund buying put spreads) on equities 
particularly attractive. This year put/call skew dropped close to historical lows across 
equity regions, sectors, and single stocks on the back of more bullish option positioning, 
making collars cheaper (Exhibit 51). Another smaller asset class that has recently begun 
to outperform is convertibles, which combine low duration with long equity calls, which 
can be beneficial in a late-cycle backdrop. Both during the late 1990s and around the 
GFC, convertibles outperformed vs. a 60/40 portfolio (Exhibit 52).

Exhibit 50: Long-dated equity calls can improve asymmetry or increase upside convexity 
Indexed total return of a portfolio of 2-year calls on S&P 500 (rolled after 1 year) + USD 1-3y IG vs. S&P 
500. Orange shade: late-cycle rallies. Blue shade: Bear markets

Source: Goldman Sachs FICC and Equities, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

(5) Looking for alternatives — private markets, commodity carry, trend following  
Allocations to alternatives can also improve risk-adjusted returns of the World 
Portfolio in a late-cycle boom. Especially when the return potential and diversification 
in traditional assets are more limited, alternatives can play a more important role. 
Exhibit 53 shows several assets and strategies that could enhance the World Portfolio 
over the medium term through either high Sharpe ratios or diversification benefits. For 
example equity/long short funds might be able to benefit from rising stock dispersion 
within the AI ecosystem. Commodity curve carry might also benefit from structurally 
higher roll yields due to commodity concentration. Finally, private markets may provide 
access to more targeted and diversified sector and style exposures than public markets 
benchmarks.

### Page 30

During periods of elevated inflation, CTAs have often delivered stronger returns, 
irrespective of inflation momentum, in contrast to traditional assets (Exhibit 54). In 
part, this is due to inflation ‘trending’ more while growth shocks happened fast 
historically. As a result, investors may underreact initially, and commodities in particular 
can trend for a long time if supported by fundamentals. Higher inflation also reduces the 
ability of central banks to buffer the business cycle and drive sharp reversals in markets. 
In the last cycle, in particular, large dovish pivots by central banks often triggered sharp 
‘risk on’ rotations during growth slowdowns. Conversely, ‘risk off’ moves, especially in 
equities, have been fast with vol of vol trending up post the GFC. Equity long/short 
hedge funds might help diversify concentrated equity benchmarks and benefit from 
rising return dispersion linked to AI winners and losers. Historically hedge funds 
outperformed their beta to the market during late cycle periods and during the Tech 
Bubble (Exhibit 55).
